Summary
This thesis focuses on advancing the theoretical understanding of isotropic α-stable
L´ evy processes, Brownian motion, and self-similar Markov processes (ssMps) through
an excursion theory framework and skew-product representation. Each chapter ad-
dresses a specific set of problems, culminating in a collection of results with theoretical
and practical significance.
In Chapter 1, we extend results on radial excursions of isotropicα-stable L´ evy processes
to Brownian motion (α = 2), deriving a Deep Wiener-Hopf factorisation and explicit ex-
pressions for ladder potential measures. Additionally, we establish the Hunt-Nagasawa
duality for isotropic α-stable processes and transient Brownian motion ( d ≥ 3), using
it to time-reverse laws at first entry and last exit of a ball.
Chapter 2 develops an excursion theory for isotropic α-stable L´ evy processes (d ≥ 2,
α ∈ (0, 2)) relative to hyperplanes, characterising key distributions such as an n-tuple
law at the moment of crossing a hyperplane. These results are extended to Brownian
motion in d ≥ 2. Additionally, conditional laws of processes attracted, repelled, or
constrained by hyperplanes are explored.
In Chapter 3, we introduce the cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu transform to represent ssMps
in Rd \ H0 as Markov Additive Processes (MAPs). This transform enables the study
of fluctuations and excursions relative to hyperplanes and slabs. The framework is
applied to isotropic α-stable processes and extends to cylindrically positive self-similar
Markov processes (cpssMps), generalising positive ssMp theory to higher dimensions.
Chapter 4 investigates first-entry and first-exit problems for isotropicα-stable processes
in higher dimensions, specifically within infinite strips. A probabilistic approach is used
to characterise the distribution of first hit or first exit to a slab. Numerical methods,
including Monte Carlo sampling algorithms, are developed to overcome the challenges
of solving integral equations in high dimensions.

Preliminaries
In this chapter, we introduce the primary tools used to develop what we refer to as
the excursion from hyperplanes framework for the isotropic α-stable L´ evy process in
dimension d ≥ 2. This process is a L´ evy process with the distinctive property that
each of its d coordinates behaves as a one-dimensional symmetric α-stable process. To
build a foundation, we begin by reviewing the relevant definitions and properties of
L´ evy processes inR.
We then explore key concepts such as path variation, regularity, hitting probabilities,
overshoot and undershoot distributions, and both point-recurrence and recurrence,
among others. These ideas are subsequently extended to the d-dimensional setting.
The chapter concludes by presenting the results from [37], which study excursions from
radial maxima and minima for the isotropic α-stable L´ evy process. These results served
as the inspiration for the excursions that give name to this thesis.
The results in all but the last section of this chapter can be found in the books of
Bertoin [4] and Kyprianou [32].
0.1 L´ evy processes
A one-dimensional L´ evy process is a stochastic process issued from the origin with
stationary and independent increments and c` adl` ag paths, whose state space isR ∪ {∂}.
The state ∂ is an absorbing state, also called killing state or cemetery. If X = (Xt :
t ≥ 0) is a one-dimensional L´ evy process with law P, there is a known expression for
its characteristic exponent Ψ, called the L´ evy-Khintchine formula, forθ ∈ R
Ψ(θ) := −1

the value q ≥ 0 is the killing rate of the process, i.e. the parameter of an independent
exponential clock which sends X to ∂. The triplet ( a, σ,Π) is called the characteristic
triplet of X.
There is an interpretation to each of the terms in the L´ evy-Khintchine formula, which
roughly speaking says that any L´ evy process can be written as the sum of three inde-
pendent process: the first one is a Brownian motion with drift, whose characteristic ex-
ponent corresponds to iaθ + 1
2 σ2θ2. The second process is a Compound Poisson process
with jumps larger than one. And the third process a limit of compensated Compound
Poisson process with jumps smaller than one. The characteristic exponent of the sum
of these two processes correspond to the term

decomposition is useful for determining some path-related behaviours, and its precise
formulation and proof can be found in Chapter 2 of [32].
Using the L´ evy-Khintchine formula, it becomes straightforward to observe that, when
the derivative Ψ′(0) := limz→0 Ψ′(z) is well-defined, differentiating gives, for t ≥ 0, the
expression
E[Xt] = iΨ′(0)t = E[X1]t. (0.1)
We refer to E[X1] as the mean of the L´ evy process, as it determines E[Xt] for any
t ≥ 0. When the mean is both well-defined and finite, together with the properties of
stationary and independent increments, (0.1) strongly suggests the occurrence of a law
of large numbers for X. This, in turn, provides insight into the long-term behaviour
of X. The following theorem presents a fairly complete picture, showing a strict tri-
chotomy: either X drifts to ∞ (limt→∞ Xt = ∞), drifts to −∞ (limt→∞ Xt = −∞), or
oscillates (lim supt→∞ Xt = − lim inft→∞ Xt = ∞).

Subordinators play a fundamental role in the theory of L´ evy processes, as many broader
results can be reduced to the analysis of appropriately selected subordinators. Their
importance is particularly evident in the Wiener-Hopf factorisation (cf. Chapter 6 of
[32]), where a L´ evy process is decomposed into the ascending and descending ladder
processes, both of which are subordinators. This factorisation is a key tool for studying
the extremal behaviour of L´ evy processes, such as the distribution of the supremum
and infimum over a time interval.
Hitting points
To explore the behaviour of the isotropic α-stable L´ evy process in relation to hyper-
planes, it is useful to first consider how a L´ evy process behaves with respect to the
probability of hitting specific points. A L´ evy process is said to hit a point x ∈ R if
P(Xt = x for at least one t > 0) > 0.
Let C = {x ∈ R : P(Xt = x for at least one t > 0) > 0} be the set of points that a
L´ evy process can hit. We say that a L´ evy process can hit points ifC ̸= ∅. We have the
following classification.

Regularity of the half-line
For a one-dimensional L´ evy processX (which starts at zero) we say that 0 is regular

say that 0 is irregular for (0 , ∞). We also say that 0 is regular for ( −∞, 0) (equiv. the

It can be shown that there exists a random measure L on [0 , ∞) with the property
that its support agrees with the closure of the set {t ≥ 0 : Xt = X t} and with
the property that, if T is any F-stopping time 1 such that XT = X T on {T < ∞},
then ((XT +t − XT , LT +t − LT ) : t ≥ 0) has the same law as (( Xt, Lt) : t ≥ 0) under P.
Moreover, the complement of its support consists of a countable union of open intervals,
each one corresponding to an excursion of X from its running maximum.
It is a straightforward exercise to show that the reflected process X − X is a strong
Markov process. The process L = (Lt : t ≥ 0), referred to as the local time at zero of the
reflected process (or equivalently, the local time of X at its maximum), has the notable
property that its inverse, ( L−1

a subordinator. This subordinator is killed if L∞ < ∞, which happens precisely when
limt→∞ Xt = −∞. Moreover, due to the regenerative nature of the point zero for the
process X − X, the random variable L∞ follows an exponential distribution (with a

the state 0 for the reflected process X − X ensures that H is a subordinator, killed at
a constant rate which may be zero (when L∞ = ∞). The process H may also possess
a drift component, corresponding to there being Lebesgue mass in the range of H.
Naturally, by considering the process −X (which is still a L´ evy process), it is imme-

0.2 One-dimensional α-stable processes
An α-stable L´ evy process,α ∈ (0, 2), is a L´ evy process X = ( Xt : t ≥ 0) with laws
(Px : x ∈ R) (this means X0 = x Px-a.s.), for which the scaling property holds, i.e. for
c > 0, (cXc−αt : t ≥ 0) under Px is equal in law to X under Pcx.
As a L´ evy process it can be defined via its characteristic triplet which takes the form
(0, 0, Π), and Π, the L´ evy measure is given by
Π(dx) = |x|−1−α 

which reduces the number of parameters from three, ( α, c1, c2) to two ( α, ρ). For the
aforesaid normalised version of X we have

∪ {α ∈ (1, 2), ρ ∈ [1 − 1/α, 1/α]}. (0.3)
In the one-dimensional setting, we will often find ourselves in future analysis dealing
with three different regimes of α hinted in (0.3) : α ∈ (0, 1), α = 1 and α ∈ (1, 2). This
is due to the different distributional and path properties for the stable process that we
detail below. For a more detailed treatment of such properties we refer the reader to
[35].
Path variation. An α-stable L´ evy process has paths of bounded variation if and only
if α ∈ (0, 1). When α ∈ [1, 2), as the associated stable process has paths of unbounded
variation, the case of monotone paths is ruled out.
Asymmetry and creeping. If the process has no negative jumps ( c2 = 0) and
α ∈ (0, 1), then necessarily ρ = 1, or in other words X must be a subordinator.
Similarly, if c1 = 0 and α ∈ (0, 1), then −X is a subordinator. A stable subordinator
has no drift. This is easy to see from the law of large numbers for L´ evy processes
and the fact that Ψ( z)/z → 0 as |z| → ∞ . Due to the lack of drift, we can say that
a stable subordinator does not creep upwards. In other words P(Xτ +
x
= x) = 0 for
τ +
x = inf{t > 0 : Xt > x}.
7
The Laplace exponent of an α-stable subordinator is given by
ψ(λ) = λα λ ≥ 0.
From the set of admissible parameters (0.3) we observe that if α ∈ (1, 2), then ρ is
bounded to be at most 1/α. This corresponds to the case c2 = 0, making the associated
process spectrally positive.
Drifting and oscillating. It is known (cf. Chapter 3 in [35]) that when α ∈ (0, 1],
stable processes have no moments. But a first moment exists whenα ∈ (1, 2). Moreover
E[Xt] = iΨ′(0)t = 0 for all t ≥ 0.
When α ∈ (0, 1] and the process experiments two-sided jumps (c1, c2 > 0), both positive
and negative parts of the L´ evy measure have a density which is proportional to 1/|x|1+α.
A straightforward application of theorem 0.1 for the limit behaviour of the process forces
us to conclude that
lim sup
t→∞
Xt = − lim inf
t→∞
Xt = ∞.
This is also the case for α ∈ (1, 2), regardless of the value of ρ.
Transience and recurrence. It can be proved that X is transient, i.e. for all ϵ > 0,
P
Z ∞
0
1{|Xt|<ϵ}dt < ∞

= 1,
whenever α ∈ (0, 1) and recurrent when α ∈ [1, 2). It is known that a recurrent process
is necessarily oscillating, confirming what Theorem 0.1 claimed previously. However,
the case of α ∈ (0, 1) is an example that an oscillating behaviour does not necessarily
imply recurrence.
Hitting points. Using Theorem 0.2 we can see that X hits points if and only if
α ∈ (1, 2).
Regularity of the half lines. If X is not a subordinator, then Theorem 0.3 establishes
that 0 is regular for (0 , ∞) and for ( −∞, 0) for α ∈ (0, 2).
Wiener-Hopf factorisation and the first passage problem

We will now consider a stable process in Rd, d ≥ 2 with a symmetry property with
respect to all axes. The notion of symmetry is then generalised by that of isotropy. All
the results considered in Chapter 2 will marginalise correctly if d = 1 is considered,
however these are well known results and for our purposes we shall always consider
d ≥ 2.
0.3 The isotropic α-stable process
We say that X = (Xt : t ≥ 0), a L´ evy process equipped with probabilities (Px : x ∈ Rd)
(Px indicates that X0 = x Px-a.s.), is a d-dimensional isotropic α-stable process if its
characteristic exponent is given by
Ψ(θ) = −1
t log E

where we write P in place of P0. Across this work we will always consider α ∈ (0, 2)
unless it is clearly indicated. The isotropic α-stable process X with α ∈ (0, 2) is a
pure-jump L´ evy process, characterised then by its jump measure Π, which satisfies
Π(B) = 2αΓ ((d + α)/2)

where B ∈ B(Rd), ℓd is the d-dimensional Lebesgue measure and σ1(dθ) is the surface
measure on Sd−1 := {x ∈ Rd : |x| = 1} normalised to have unit mass. The changes in
the constant come from the Jacobian in the change of coordinate system.
Additionally to the properties of a L´ evy process, the α-stable process possesses the
following self-similarity property. For any c > 0 and x ∈ Rd, the law of (cXc−αt : t ≥ 0)
under Px, is that of X under Pcx. The isotropy of X means that for every orthogonal
transformation U : Rd → Rd and x ∈ Rd, the law of
(U Xt : t ≥ 0) under Px is equal to the law of ( Xt : t ≥ 0) under PU x.
Observe that using isotropy it is possible to translate an event originally stated for
10
a given hyperplane Hv = {x ∈ Rd : ⟨x, v⟩ = 0 }, into an event now regarding the
hyperplane H0 = {x ∈ Rd : x(1) = 0}, where x(1) represents the first coordinate of x.
An example of a L´ evy process process with the self-similarity and isotropy properties is
the d-dimensional Brownian motion. The characteristic exponent of Brownian motion
is given by the function (0.8) taking α = 2. We will separate the study of Brownian
motion and present it separately due to the continuity of its paths. This is the reason
why many of the techniques used for stable processes do not extend in a simple way to
the Brownian case.
To describe how the paths of X behave in relation to hyperplanes and other related
domains in Rd we need to point out that a subset of k ∈ { 1, 2, . . . , d} coordinates of
X, under Px, is in fact a k-dimensional isotropic α-stable L´ evy process. This follows
by evaluating (0.8) on the vector θk := (θ(1), θ(2), . . . , θ(k), 0, . . . ,0) and noting that
Ψ(θk) = |θk|α.
In particular X(1) is a symmetric real valued α-stable process. By using isotropy,
the previous property about the coordinates of X and recalling some path properties
of symmetric real-valued stable processes from the previous section we deduce the
following.
• The isotropic α-stable process touches H0 (and, by isotropy, any other hyper-
plane) in finite time with positive probability when α ∈ (1, 2) and with proba-
bility zero when α ∈ (0, 1]. A similar story holds if we replace H0 by the sphere
Sd−1. In that case if α ∈ (0, 1) the process will not hit the unit sphere in finite
time but if 1 < α < d there is a positive probability of hitting the sphere in finite
time. The latter assertion was proved in [47] by studying the renewal measure of
the radial process |X| = (|Xt| : t ≥ 0).
• Although X is transient in the sense that limt→∞ |Xt| = ∞, due to the oscillation
of X(1), i.e. lim sup t→∞ Xt = − lim inft→∞ Xt = +∞, for any x ∈ Rd the process
Px-almost surely visits H0↓ = {z ∈ Rd : z(1) < 0} and H0↑ = {z ∈ Rd : z(1) > 0}
the ‘lower’ and ‘upper’ half-spaces an infinite number of times.
• The process X started in x = (x(1), . . . , x(d)) is regular for both the half spaces
(x(1), ∞) × Rd−1 and (−∞, x(1)) × Rd−1. This is, for τ ∧
x(1) := inf {t > 0 : X(1) >
x(1)} and τ ∨
x(1) := inf {t > 0 : X(1) < x (1)}, we necessarily have Px(τ ∧
x(1) = 0) =
Px(τ ∨
x(1) = 0) = 1.
11
• Crossing a hyperplane does not occur by creeping. This means, for r ̸= x(1) and
τ ∨
r = inf{t > 0 : X(1)
t < r } and τ ∧
r = inf{t > 0 : X(1)
t > r }
Px(X(1)
τ ∨r
< r) = Px(X(1)
τ ∧r
> r) = 1.
With all these properties in mind we will develop a theory of excursions from a di-
rectional minimum/maximum. These are excursions ‘indexed’ on the set of times at
which the process reaches a new minimum in the direction of X(1). By isotropy, these
excursions work analogously for the maximum in the direction of X(1) and for extrema
in any other given direction.
The excursion theory framework we are about to introduce is applicable to Markov Ad-
ditive Processes. These processes encompass a variety of cases, including the isotropic
α-stable L´ evy process seen asX = (X(1), X(2:d)) and the process underlying the skew-
product representation of the isotropic α-stable process, known as the Lamperti-Kiu
transform, among others.
0.4 Excursions of Markov Additive Processes
Before introducing the definition of Markov additive processes (MAPs) let us intro-
duce the notion of Feller process. Consider E a locally compact Hausdorff space with
countable base, such as Rd, and C0(E) be the Banach space of real-valued continuous
bounded functions on E which decay to 0 as ∥x∥ → ∞ equipped with the supremum
norm. Recall that an E-valued Markov process X defined in some completed, filtered
probability space (Ω , F , (Ft)t≥0) with laws ( Px : x ∈ E) is said to be a Feller process
if its semigroup Pt[f](x) := Ex [f(Xt)] satisfies the so-called Feller properties. That is,
for any f ∈ C0(E),
1. for all t ≥ 0 Pt[f] ∈ C0(E), and
2. for all x ∈ E, limt↓0 Pt[f](x) = f(x).
An important consequence of X being Feller is that it is also strongly Markovian and
admits a modification that is both c` adl` ag and quasi-left-continuous. The latter property
means that for any increasing sequence of stopping times ( Tn)n≥0 with respect to the
filtration ( Ft)t≥0, converging to a stopping time T , we have X(Tn) → X(T ) almost
surely on the event {T < ∞}. We will refer to this modification as a regular Feller
process. For more details the reader is referred to Section III.2 of [51].
We say that (Ξ , Υ) = ((Ξ t, Υt), t ≥ 0) is a Markov additive process if it is a Feller
12
process on R × S, with probabilities (Px,θ : x ∈ R, θ ∈ S ), where S is a locally compact
space with countable base, such that, for any t ≥ 0, the conditional law of the process
((Ξs+t − Ξt, Υs+t) : s ≥ 0), given ((Ξ u, Υu), u ≤ t) is that of (Ξ , Υ) under P0,θ, with
θ = Υt. For a MAP (Ξ , Υ) we call Ξ the ordinate and Υ the modulator.
For instance, the isotropicα-stable L´ evy processX, split coordinate-wise as (X(1), X(2:d)),
constitutes a MAP with ordinate X(1) and modulator X(2:d). This is a simple conse-
quence of the independence and stationarity of the increments of X.
The following example of a Markov additive process, related to isotropic α-stable pro-
cesses through the so-called Lamperti-Kiu transform, will help us to understand more
general excursions for MAPs and their consequences. We will present some of the re-
sults of [37], product of a theory of excursions from the radial minimum of X. These
results are the inspiration for the development of excursions from directional extrema.
The Lamperti-Kiu transform
According to [1], there exists a MAP ( ξ, Θ) with probabilities

Remark 0.6. Consider the Lamperti-Kiu transform (0.10) and notice that the ex-
cursions of (U, Θ) from {0} × Sd−1 translate into radial excursions for the process
Yt := eξtΘt, t ≥ 0. Ignoring the time change φ(t) we see that the radial minima of the
process Y agree with the radial minima of the stable process X.
Remark 0.7. The excursion framework constructed above can be used for the isotropic
α-stable process X = ( X(1), X(2:d)) seen as a MAP. In this case S = Rd−1 and the
excursions of X from the minimum/maximum of X(1) are called directional excursions
on account that they can be generalised to any direction due to isotropy. The only
requirement to be addressed is the regularity of X(1) with respect to the upper/lower
half-lines, which is guaranteed by Theorem 0.3.

In following chapters we use excursions from the minimum/maximum of various MAPs.
For all of them we will only specify the notation for descending/ascending processes,
local time, and excursion measure. The excursion framework is crucial for all of the
results in this work.
0.5 Results on excursions from the radial minimum of
stable processes
In [37], the third paper in a series of papers [31, 38, 37] focused on studying Wiener-
Hopf factorisations for the α-stable L´ evy process, the authors consider the isotropic
α-stable process ( X, P) in Rd, d ≥ 2, α ∈ (0, 2). They develop the excursion theory
from the radial minimum we showed above, and proved the following series of results.

addressed in [38].

Remark 0.11. Equation (0.23) ‘factorises’ the behaviour (seen as a resolvent) of the
MAP underlying X into its ascending and descending components, where ascending is
interpreted in terms of increasing radial distance. This is somewhat analogous to the
real-valued L´ evy process case (see Chapter 6 of [32]), where the characteristic exponent
is factorised into two characteristic exponents, those of the ascending and descending
ladder processes. We refer to this decomposition of operators as a deep factorisation.
Remark 0.12. The factorisation (0.23) is the first example of a Wiener-Hopf factori-
sation for a symmetric Markov Additive Process with a continuous state space ( Sd−1)
for the modulator. Previous references to this type of factorisations, albeit for the case
of modulators with finite state space, can be found in in Theorem (3.28) of [25] or in
Chapter IX of [3]. In particular for the MAPs associated to the Lamperti-Kiu trans-
form of real self-similar Markov processes, [31] and [38] present the Deep Wiener-Hopf
factorisation and applications.
20
Chapter 1
Contributions to the radial
analysis of Isotropic stable L´ evy
processes
In the first part of this chapter we extend the results of [37] on radial excursions of the
isotropic α-stable L´ evy process to the case of Brownian Motion (α = 2). Our main re-
sult will be a Deep Wiener-Hopf factorisation in the sense of Theorem 0.10. Many other
identities will be obtained during the process of proving said factorisation, remarkably,
explicit expressions for ascending and descending ladder potential measures. We will
note, however, that not all the results from [37] make sense for Brownian motion, due
to the continuity of its paths.
In the second part of the chapter, we establish and prove the Hunt-Nagasawa duality for
isotropic α-stable processes and transient Brownian motion ( d ≥ 3), both conditioned
on hitting a specified point. This duality is then used to time reverse the so-called
triple law at first entry/exit of a ball, and finding a triple law at the last hit inside the
ball.
1.1 Brownian motion’s radial ladder potential measures
We will start by recalling that a Brownian motion Y = ( Yt : t ≥ 0) in Rd, d ≥ 3
with laws ( Px : x ∈ Rd) (case d = 2 will be treated in Subsection 1.1.2) admits the
21
Lamperti-Kiu representation (0.10), i.e.

ξ and Θ are independent processes; ξ is a Brownian motion with drift, the Lamperti
transform of a Bessel( d), and Θ is a Brownian motion on Sd−1 (cf. Chapter VII of [22]
and [57]). It is well known that, for Y , points in Rd are polar and lim t→∞ |Yt| = ∞.
This ensures that, when issued from x ̸= 0, there exists a point of closest reach to the
origin and the instant of closest reach can be denoted by

Our second main result is the deep Wiener-Hopf factorisation of Y which extends
Theorem 0.10 to the case α = 2. To do this we need to define the ascending and
descending ladder potential measures. Recall that Y is regular for the interior and
exterior of any sphere Sd−1
r for r > 0. Hence, a pair ( L, n−), where L is a local time at
the radial minimum and n− are the excursion measures, exists and can be defined in
the same way of the previous section. Consequently, the MAPs ( ξ−, Θ−) and (ξ+, Θ+)
are defined in the obvious way, and one can consider the potential measures U −

and U+(dy) as defined in (0.18) and (0.22) but for the Brownian case.

In this case, the Deep Wiener-Hopf factorisation decomposes the resolvent of the MAP
underlying Y —expressed as an integral with respect to the Newtonian potential—into
two resolvents corresponding to the ascending and descending ladder potentials, where
ascending and descending refer to increases or decreases in radial distance. As in the
α-stable case, the ascending and descending ladder MAPs are related by a duality
principle. The specific duality relation employed is detailed in the proof.

Helped by Proposition 1.5, we can now prove the Deep Wiener-Hopf factorisation.

So far we have only discussed the case d ≥ 3, for which most of the techniques used in

motion, can be stated as consequence of what we call excursions from hyperplanes ,
or excursions from directional minimum/maximum, see Remark 0.7. In the following
subsection we will only present the results, while the proofs will be a particular case of
those in the next chapter.
1.1.2 Deep Factorisation of Planar Brownian Motion
In the two-dimensional case, the Lamperti representation (0.10) of a planar Brownian
motion Y with laws (Px : x ∈ R2 \ {0}) can be posed in the following way,
Yt = eξφ(t)+iθφ(t), t ≥ 0,
where θ corresponds to the windings around the origin. The ordinate ξ is to be inter-
preted in the usual way, as the norm of the process and in this particular case, ξ and
θ turn out to be independent Brownian motions (see Section 7.15 in [22]).
From this we have that lim sup t→∞ ξt = − lim inft→∞ ξt = ∞, which implies that
when started from x ̸= 0 the point of closest reach to the origin is the origin itself.
31
However, helped by the independence of ξ and θ, we can find the potential measure of
the ascending and descending ladder MAPs.
For simplicity in dealing with the deep Wiener-Hopf factorisation, we will not use the
same definition of potential measures we used in the d ≥ 3 case. It is enough to point
out that excursions from the minimum/maximum radius of Y translate into excursions
from the minimum/maximum in the direction of ξ of the planar BM ( ξ, θ).
It is well known (cf. Section 6.5 of [32]) that the inverse local time at the maximum for
ξ, L−1, is a 1
2-stable subordinator and ξ+ and −ξ− are unit-rate linear drifts. Hence,
given the independence of θ and L−1, the ascending ladder MAP ( ξ+
t , θL−1

law to (t, St) where ( St)t≥0 is a Cauchy process.

1.2.1 Hunt-Nagasawa duality and conditioned processes
We will start by introducing the isotropic α-stable process, α ∈ (0, 2), conditioned to
continuously hit a point θ ∈ Rd. We will preemptively denote this process by (X, P{θ}).

same paper it is said that P{θ} cannot be contextualised using the limiting description
used for hitting patches on the sphere. However, we will show that the conditioning is
simple, coincides with PS when S = {θ} and, more importantly, is valid for α ∈ (0, 2).

Chapter 2
Directional excursions of stable
processes
For the isotropic α-stable L´ evy process in dimensiond ≥ 2 and α ∈ (0, 2), we present
a theory of excursions from the minimum/maximum of the process with respect to a
direction given by a normal vector v. For this construction, explicit expressions for
the potential measures of the ladder processes are obtained, as well as other important
excursion-related functionals. Applications of this excursion theory allow us to charac-
terise the joint law of the process at three key random times, the instant of closest reach
to a hyperplane before crossing it, the time of crossing the hyperplane and the moment
right before the crossing. We call this result the triple law. An identity in the spirit of
the so-called Deep Wiener-Hopf factorisation is also proved. At the end of the chapter
we prove that after some light adjustments, we can follow the same methodology to
find the ascending and descending potential measures and a Deep Wiener-Hopf-type
factorisation for Brownian motion of dimension d ≥ 2. The analysis of a point of closest
reach to a hyperplane is not pertinent for Brownian motion, since it hits half-spaces
continuously. Finally, by using the distribution of the point of closest reach to a hy-
perplane, we define and characterise the conditional law of the process to be attracted
to a subset of a given hyperplane by one-side of the hyperplane, the conditional law of
the process which never visits a half-space and the conditional law of a process which
is repelled from a hyperplane.
41
2.1 Introduction
The study of fluctuation identities for α-stable L´ evy processes was immensely benefited
from the discoveries of Lamperti (cf. [40]) of a bijection between the class of positive
self-similar Markov process and L´ evy processes. Many articles use the so-called Lam-
perti transform to solve, in an intuitive probabilistic way, a variety of questions which
in the past were often solved by purely analytic methods. See for instance [13] [39]
[36]. More recently, the generalistation of the Lamperti transform, to a relationship
between real-valued self-similar process and Markov Additive Processes (MAPs), a type
of modulated L´ evy processes, has given birth to various results on the study not only
of the α-stable process on R but also the isotropic α-stable process in dimension d ≥ 2.
In 2019, Kyprianou et al. [37], used the Lamperti-Kiu transform to develop a theory
of excursions from the radial minimum, for the isotropic stable process in dimension
d ≥ 2. With this, they found the law of the point of radial closest reach to the origin,
and exhibit the so-called deep factorisation for the Lamperti-Kiu-transform MAP (see
Chapter 1). The characterisation of the point of closest reach allows, among other
things, to make sense of the asymptotic conditioning of a process to continuously touch
a set on the surface of the unitary sphere. This conditioning was studied in [34], and
it opened the door to the question: can such conditioning be done if the sphere is
replaced by a hyperplane? Simultaneously, in [28], the law of the process conditioned
to touch a set on the surface of the sphere was also found. This time, in contrast
to the conditioning in [34], the process is allowed to visit the interior of the unit ball
an infinite number of times before touching said set, and did not need the theory of
the radial point of closest reach. In this chapter the case of the hyperplane was also
considered, contributing to the curiosity in characterising the dynamics of the stable
process in relation to hyperplanes or half-spaces.
Other recent publications also highlight the relevance of investigations regarding L´ evy
processes in coordinate systems based on the projection onto an arbitrary hyperplane
and its orthogonal direction. In [23], the authors investigate the decomposition of the
trajectories of a L´ evy processY in Rd splitting into what happens before and after the
process ⟨Y, v⟩ = (⟨Yt, v⟩ : t ≥ 0) reaches a minimum (here v is a fixed normal vector
in Rd). Another recent publication in regards stable processes and hyperplanes is [44],
where the authors found a formula for the Laplace transform of the first hitting time
to a hyperplane, and the distribution of the first hit on such hyperplane.
42
2.2 Results on excursions
The following results are consequences of the development of a theory of excursions
from the minimum of X in the direction of e 1 = (1, 0, . . . ,0). The research conducted
in this direction has fundamentally followed the same steps of the theory of excursions
from the radial minimum addressed in [37]. The main differences are the distributional
properties of the radial process |X| = ( |Xt| : t ≥ 0) versus those of X(1), a one-
dimensional α-stable process. These differences present enough technical differences to
dedicate a detailed treatment of the procedure.
The main results of this section for the isotropic α-stable process in Rd where d ≥ 2 and
α ∈ (0, 2) are Theorems 2.1, 2.3 and 2.7 presented below, and their proofs are provided
in Subsection 2.2.1. In Subsection 2.2.2 we prove that some of the formulas for the
α-stable process with α ∈ (0, 2), in particular the Deep Wiener-Hopf factorisation
(Theorem 2.9), are also valid for the case of Brownian motion, although the methods
used to prove this are somewhat different from those used in the first part.
Theorem 2.1 (Law of the one-sided point of closest reach to the hyperplane H0).
Let x, y ∈ H0↑ = {z ∈ Rd : z(1) > 0}, G(t) := sup {s < t : X(1)
s = inf u<t X(1)
u }
denote the last instant before t where X reaches a minimum in its first coordinate, and
τ ∨
0 := inf {s > 0 : X(1)
s < 0}, the time of first passage below H0 = {z ∈ Rd : z(1) = 0},
then
Px(XG(τ ∨
0 ) ∈ dy) = Cα,d
|y(1) − x(1)|α/2
|y(1)|α/2 |x − y|−ddy for 0 < y (1) < x(1), (2.1)
where Cα,d = π−(d/2+1)Γ(d/2) sin(απ/2).
The ‘one-sided’ qualification of this random variable refers to the fact that the direc-
tional minimum is recorded while the process remains in the upper half-space in which
the hyperplane H0 separated the space. The concept of one-sided point of closest reach
was born from the analogy to the point of closest distance to the origin, and it is the
building block of the conditional law to approach a subset of the hyperplane H0 from
one side. We will return to this conditional law at the end of this chapter.
Remark 2.2. If we consider x, y ∈ H0↓ = {z ∈ Rd : z(1) < 0} such that x(1) <
y(1) < 0, replace τ ∨

H0) by symmetry, the kernel in (2.1) is also the density of XG(τ ∧

The use of an excursion framework to characterise directional minima, together with
explicit expressions for functionals under the excursion measure, allow us to obtain
joint laws of relevant quantities at first passage times in regards half-spaces. These
n-tuple laws at first entry/exit of a half-space provide a non-trivial extension to the
simple undershoot-overshoot laws.
Theorem 2.3 (Triple law at the first crossing of a hyperplane) . Fix r ∈ R and define
for w, x, y ∈ Hr↑ = {x ∈ Rd : x(1) > r } and z ∈ Hr↓ = {x ∈ Rd : x(1) < r }, the
following density
Ξw(x, y, z) := Aα,d
|w(1) − x(1)|α/2
|w − x|d
|x(1) − y(1)|α/2
|x − y|d |y − z|−α−d
where Aα,d =
2αΓ(d/2)2Γ( d+α
2 )
π3d/2Γ(α/2)2|Γ(−α/2)|. Finally, let G(τ ∨
r ) denote the instant of closest reach
to the hyperplane Hr before first entry into Hr↓. Then, for w(1) > x(1) > r, y (1) > x(1)
and z(1) < r,
Pw(XG(τ ∨r ) ∈ dx, Xτ ∨r − ∈ dy, Xτ ∨r ∈ dz) = Ξw(x, y, z) dx dy dz. (2.2)
Once again, by symmetry, the previous result can be easily extended to the case where
the point of issue is in Hr↓ and the upward crossing occurs at τ ∧
r . This is, for w(1) <
x(1) < r, y (1) < x(1) and z(1) > r,
Pw(XG(τ ∧r ) ∈ dx, Xτ ∧r − ∈ dy, Xτ ∧r ∈ dz) = Ξw(x, y, z) dx dy dz.
Technically speaking, the next two lemmas could be obtained by marginalising (2.2),
and thus may be viewed as corollaries of Theorem 2.3. However, they can also be
derived directly through a more straightforward approach. For this reason, we present
them as standalone lemmas and provide direct proofs in Subsection 2.2.1.

Remark 2.6. Lemma 2.5 is known already from [12] and Lemma 2.4 is also implicitly
known from the same reference, albeit stated up to multiplication by the stable jump
measure as the Green function for X killed on exiting Hr↑. The approach taken by [12]
uses potential analysis rather than the fluctuation theory approach that we use here.
In the same spirit of the so-called deep Wiener-Hopf factorisation for the radial MAP
(ξ, Θ) in the Lamperti-Kiu representation of X, we exhibit a deep factorisation for
the orthogonal decomposition ( ⟨X, e1⟩, Xe⊥
1
) = (X(1), X(2:d)), which, as pointed out in
Remark 0.7, turns out to be a MAP. An analogous relationship to the Deep Wiener-
Hopf factorisation in the radial case (in Theorem 0.10) turns out to be true for this
decomposition. It is worth mentioning that in [53], the potential measure of a L´ evy
process killed in a half-space is factorised in a pair of measures associated to a path
decomposition at the time of a directional minimum. We show that for the isotropic
α-stable process, those measures are in fact the ascending and descending potential
ladder measures, giving them a simple probabilistic meaning. Our derivation of the
potentials also follows a different approach, based on theory of excursions.
To state the deep Wiener-Hopf factorisation result we require to define the (d-dimensional)
ascending and descending ladder processes in the direction of X(1). Recall the frame-
work of excursions in Section 0.4, in the particular case of Remark 0.7, consider

2.2.1 Proofs of results for α ∈ (0, 2)
Proofs of Lemmas 2.4 and 2.5
In order to prove Lemmas 2.4 and 2.5 we use a method of converting results for first
entry into a ball into first entry into a half space by expanding the radius of the sphere
asymptotically so that, with re-centring, the interior of the ball becomes a half-space.
We call this the flat earth theory , in the sense that, from an infinitesimally localised
perspective, the surface of a sphere appears to be that of a flat surface.
The lemma we present below combines a geometric argument with the L´ evy and scaling
properties of the stable process ( X, P).

Remark 2.11. Before starting the proof of the fundamental results in this chapter,
we address the rather obvious question as to why flat Earth theory cannot be used to
address Theorem 2.1. In theory this may well be possible but we could not see a way
to ensure that the point of closest reach to the origin preceding first entry to the ball
B(−Re1,R+r) asymptotically converges to XG(τ ∨r ). For this reason, we take a different
approach to proving Theorem 2.1, which uses the so-called directional excursion as
defined in Remark 0.7.

2.2.2 Proofs for Brownian motion
Consider a Brownian motion Y = (Yt : t ≥ 0) in dimension d ≥ 3 with laws ( Px : x ∈
Rd).
Due to the regularity of 0 with respect to (0, ∞) and (−∞, 0) for Y (1), the construction
of a local time at a directional minimum/maximum is analogous to that one done in
Section 0.4. It is clear then that an adequate choice of local time for indexing excur-
sions from the directional minimum is the local time at zero for Y (1) − Y (1), denoted
by L = (Lt : t ≥ 0). Let L−1 be its right-continuous inverse. By the independence of
Y (1) and Y (2:d), L and L−1 are also independent of Y (2:d).
There are additional properties of Brownian local times that we can take advantage
of. In order to state them let us first define the ascending/descending ladder height
processes. We define ( H −, Y (2:d)−) =

H −
t , Y (2:d)−
t : t ≥ 0

2.3 Results on conditional laws
2.3.1 Touching the hyperplane from one side
The following results are an extension of those in [34], where the law of the point of
closest reach to the origin XG(∞) is used to condition the isotropic α-stable L´ evy pro-
cess (X, P) to continuously approach a set on the unit sphere. In this case we will use
the law of the point of closest reach to a hyperplane before crossing such hyperplane .
The explicit expression of the law of XG(τ ∨
0 ) was explicitly obtained in Subsection 2.2.
69
Consider S, a subset of H0 = {z ∈ Rd : z(1) = 0 }, such that it has strictly positive
Lebesgue measure ℓd−1 on H0 or it is a point. We aim to make sense of the law
of X conditioned to approach S continuously from the half-space H0↑. We will give
a probabilistic construction of such conditional probability via a limiting procedure.
Moreover, this approach allows us to identify this law as a Doob h-transform of the
stable process killed when first entering H0↓.
Let us formalise the idea of conditioning the process X to be attracted to S from H0↑.
When S is not a point, let us define for ϵ > 0 Sϵ = (0 , ϵ) × π(2:d)(S), where π(2:d)
refers to the projection function from Rd to Rd−1 that suppresses the first coordinate
component. Consider the event C∨

0 ) ∈ Sϵ} where G(τ ∨

2.3.2 Oscillatory attraction/repulsion to a hyperplane
In [28], the isotropic d-dimensional α-stable process with α ∈ (0, 1] and d ≥ 2 (which
would not hit a hyperplane) is conditioned to continuously approach a subset D of the
hyperplane H0. It is shown in Theorem 1 therein that this conditioning corresponds
to a Doob h-transform, where the h function is MD(x) =

equivalent to conditioning the process to be repelled from zero. We will show that
when d ≥ 2 and α ∈ (1, 2), the h-transform (2.64) is equivalent to condition the

Chapter 3
Self-similar Markov processes
and Cylindrical MAPs
In this chapter we introduce a new representation of self-similar Markov processes on
the space E := Rd \ H0 = {z ∈ Rd : z(1) ̸= 0} in terms of Markov Additive Processes
(MAPs). If X = (X(1), X(2:d)) is a self-similar Markov process onE, this representation
consists of the classical Lamperti-Kiu transformation applied toX(1) (cf. [38]), but with
an extended modulator which now accounts not only for the sign of X(1) but also for
X(2:d). We refer to this representation as the cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu transform, and
the corresponding transformed process as a cylindrical MAP.
We will explore various examples of self-similar Markov processes on Rd \ H0. In the
first section, we focus on the isotropic α-stable process killed upon its first hit into H0.
For this process, we will identify the corresponding MAP and describe it through its
L´ evy system. Leveraging this, we will show a space-time transformation of the stable
process whose cylindrical MAP serves as a dual MAP, akin to the Riesz-Bogdan- ˙Zak
transform. Additionally, we will develop an excursion theory and apply it to express
certain fluctuations of the stable process relative to hyperplanes and slabs in terms of
ladder processes associated with their cylindrical MAP transforms.
The second section is devoted to examples of ssMPs on H0↑ = {z ∈ Rd : z(1) > 0},
which we refer to as cylindrically positive self-similar Markov processes (cpssMps).
These processes will be constructed from the isotropic α-stable process through killing,
censoring, reflecting, and conditioning, thereby generalising the notion of positive self-
similar Markov processes to higher dimensions. For each example, we will present their
cylindrical MAP and employ excursion theory to derive alternative formulations of in-
81
teresting fluctuation identities for their self-similar counterpart. In some cases, explicit
calculations will be provided, particularly for certain cpssMps related to Brownian
motion.
The benefits of using the cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu transform stem from its ability to
facilitate the study of ssMP fluctuations within the framework of MAPs, a theory that
has seen significant development in recent years (cf. [30], [29]).
3.1 Introduction
The construction of positive self-similar Markov processes (pssMps) by killing, censor-
ing, reflecting or conditioning an α-stable L´ evy process onR is undoubtedly an effective
technique used in understanding the behaviour of the stable process (cf. Chapter V
of [35]). Roughly speaking, this technique consists of finding an appropriate embed-
ded pssMp and ‘translating’ a question for the one-dimensional stable process which
is difficult to solve, (e.g. the distribution of a first hit into a given set, calculation of
potentials, conditional laws of the process or entrance laws) into a simpler question
regarding the Lamperti transform of the identified pssMp. A good example of this is
found in [36], where the authors find an explicit expression for the distribution of the
first hit into the interval (0 , 1), by showing that this corresponds to the distribution
of the first passage below 1 for a ‘censored’ α-stable L´ evy process, which conveniently
erases the negative parts of its paths. After proving that the censored process is indeed
a pssMp, it is possible to solve the distribution of the first passage below 1 by relating
it to the first passage below zero of its Lamperti transform.
With the generalisation of the Lamperti transform to represent real-valued self-similar
Markov processes or d-dimensional self-similar processes in terms of MAPs (cf. [1]),
the previous technique has been extended to consider problems in these more general
spaces. For instance, it was used in [37] to answer questions of interest in Potential
theory which have been addressed in the previous chapters of this thesis.
Our cylindrical transform builds on MAP representations of self-similar Markov pro-
cesses and appears to be more suitable for studying questions related to stable processes
and domains defined by x(1), such as half-spaces, hyperplanes, or infinite strips. Later
in this chapter, we will explore fluctuations of α-stable L´ evy processes concerning these
sets, using various examples of path transformations that lead us to work with cylin-
drically positive self-similar Markov processes.
82
3.2 The Cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu transform
Before presenting the main result of this chapter let us define precisely the notion of
self-similar Markov process X in E. A regular Feller process X (see Section 0.4 for the
definition) with laws P as described above will be called a self-similar Markov process
(of index α) if there exists a constant α > 0 such that, for any x ∈ Rd \ H0 and c > 0,
the law of ( cXc−αt : t ≥ 0) under Px is Pcx,
where Px is the law of X when issued from x.
We will generally omit to mention (Ω , F , (Ft)t≥0) and in what follows, a process satis-
fying the properties above will be denoted by ( X, P). An additional consideration to
be made is that we will allow the process X to be sent to an additional absorbing state
† when X hits H0. The time ζc = inf {t > 0 : Xt ∈ H0} will be called the lifetime
of the process and we must notice that absorption may or may not hold with positive
probability.
Also, see Section 0.4 for the definition of a Markov Additive Process ( ξ, Θ). In the
same way we did consider possibly killed self-similar Markov processes, we will now
consider the possibility for a MAP of being sent to an absorbing state δ = (δξ, δΘ) and
will denote this lifetime by ζM AP.
In contrast to the radial Lamperti-Kiu transform (0.10) where the ordinate represents,
up to a time-change, the behaviour of log |X| and the modulator corresponds arg(X) =
X/|X|, in our main result below we present a MAP representation of X in which the
ordinate corresponds a time change of log |X(1)|, and the correlation structure between
X(1) and X(2:d) is encoded in a modulator which in some sense represents, additionally
to the sign of X(1), the position of the (2 : d) coordinates over the plane Rd−1 under a
scaling by |X(1)|.

3.2.1 L´ evy systems
We will now outline how certain MAPs derived from the cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu of
self-similar Markov processes on E can be characterised by examining their jump rates.
The key tool for this characterisation is the concept of L´ evy System, represented by
the pair ( H, Π). Here, H is an additive functional that tracks the dynamics of the
modulator, while Π is a transition kernel that, conditionally on the current state of the
modulator, describes the instantaneous jump rate of the ordinate.
We need to assume that the modulator ( Jt, Ξt)t≥0 in the cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu
transform is a Hunt process, this is, among other conditions, asking that ( J, Ξ) is
quasi-left continuous on [0 , ∞) (see Chapter 1 of [7] for a precise definition of Hunt
processes), and note that by definition ( ξt)t≥0 is quasi-left continuous on [0 , ζM AP).
It was shown in [18] that for MAPs with these conditions there exists a continuous
increasing additive functional t 7→ Ht of (J, Ξ) and a transition kernel Π from {−1, 1} ×
Rd−1 to
 
{−1, 1} × Rd−1

Π ((j, θ), {(j, θ), 0}) = 0,

It was proved in Theorem 2.23 of [19] that similar to the L´ evy-Itˆ o decomposition of
L´ evy processes we can decompose ξ as the addition of three types of conditionally
independent processes: an additive functional, a Gaussian process and a purely discon-
tinuous process. If all the dynamics of ξ are explained by its purely discontinuous part
then it is clear that the jump measure in the L´ evy system characterises the MAP. That
is the case of (ξ, (J, Ξ)), the MAP corresponding to the cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu trans-
form of the isotropic α-stable process, for wihch we can calculate Π explicitly. Some
references on the study of MAPs and L´ evy systems are [18, 17, 24, 3, 30] however, the
87
reader must be aware that most of the examples of MAPs in these texts consider the
modulator to be a Markov chain.
The Lamperti-stable cylindrical MAP
Let us consider X = (Xt : t ≥ 0), an isotropic α-stable L´ evy process with laws (Px :
x ∈ Rd \ H0). This process is self-similar of index α ∈ (0, 2). And defined up to its
lifetime τ{0} := inf {t > 0 : Xt ∈ H0}, by Theorem 3.1 there exists a MAP ( ξ, (J, Ξ))
with laws
 
P(y,j,θ) : y ∈ R, j ∈ {−1, 1}, θ ∈ Rd−1
such that equation (3.2) holds. We
will call the process ( ξ, (J, Ξ)) the Lamperti-stable cylindrical MAP.
Notice that the killing time τ{0} depends only on the behaviour of ( X(1)
t : t ≥ 0) as
a marginal of X, and by the hitting-points properties of real-valued stable process,
this implies that the stable cylindrical MAP is killed in finite time only if α ∈ (1, 2).
Conversely, when α ∈ (0, 1] the MAP does not experience killing.
The pure-jump behaviour of the α-stable process causes the Lamperti-stable cylindrical
MAP to possess a pure-jump behaviour, i.e., for any t > 0 the whole dynamics of the
process will be governed by the jump measure of the MAP. Notice from the expression
of the L´ evy measure of X, equation (0.9), that co-planar jumps do not occur P-a.s.
This implies that the modulator ( J, Ξ) and the ordinate ξ jump together. In the case
of (J, Ξ), the jump may be in Ξ only or in both ( J, Ξ) but never of J solely.
The jump measure of the stable cylindrical MAP is calculated in the following proposi-
tion. The MAP property is reflected in the fact that the jump measure is independent
of the current position of the ordinateξ, and just depends on the state of the modulator.
Proposition 3.3 (L´ evy system of the stable cylindrical MAP) . Let (X, P) be the
isotropic α-stable process in Rd killed when it first hits H0, and (ξ, (J, Ξ)) its asso-
ciated cylindrical MAP. Then, for a bounded measurable function f : [0, ∞) × R × R ×
{−1, 1} × {−1, 1} × Rd−1 × Rd−1 → R such that f(·, ·, 0, ·, ·, ·, ·) = 0 and i, θ in {−1, 1}
and Rd−1 respectively,

3.2.3 Fluctuation identities for the stable cylindrical MAP
There are a series of fluctuation identities for the α-stable process which emerge natu-
rally from the cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu representation. These fluctuations are higher-
dimensional analogues of identities investigated in [36, 38, 52], and, to the best of our
knowledge, they do not have an explicit formula. However, in this section we will rein-
terpret them in terms of the cylindrical stable MAP and finally express them in terms
of potential ladder measures and excursion functionals which provide insight into their
analytic structure.
The excursions will be a particular case of those developed in Section 0.4. In the
91
radial MAP (0.10) the MAP modulator represents either the sign of the stable process
when d = 1, or the angular position in polar coordinates when d ≥ 2. In our case the
modulator contains the information of the sign ofX(1) and the position of the remaining
(2 : d) scaled by |X(1)|, hence the excursions from the minimum or maximum of ξ will
represent, for the stable process, excursions from the minimal/maximal distance to H0
(see Figure 3-1).
To work under a framework of excursions from the minimum ofξ for the MAP (ξ, (J, Ξ))
we appeal to Section 0.4 just pointing out that 1 is regular for (1 , ∞) and ( −∞, 1) for
X(1), hence 0 is regular for both ( −∞, 0) and (0 , ∞) for ξ, and we can consider a
continuous additive functional L = ( Lt : t ≥ 0) to be the local time at zero for the
ordinate reflected at its minimumξ−ξ. Therefore, it is possible to define the descending
ladder MAP ( H −
t , (J −, Ξ−)t)t≥0 by the relationship

3.3 Cylindrically positive self-similar Markov processes
In this section we will present examples of self-similar processes in H0↑ and their re-
spective cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu transform. The restriction to the upper half-space,
in terms of their cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu transform, implies that J ≡ 1, reason why
we will omit J in the notation for the modulator of the MAP. We call these processes
cylindrically positive self-similar Markov processes and they generalise the notion of
positive ssMps to greater dimensions. In terms of their transforms, they pass from
being a L´ evy process to be a Markov Additive Process with a modulator space Rd−1.
The purpose of this section is to define specific cpssMps derived from both the isotropic
95
α-stable L´ evy process and Brownian motion, and demonstrate how their cylindrical
Lamperti-Kiu transform enables the application of excursion theory for MAPs. This
approach allows us to reinterpret interesting fluctuations of stable L´ evy processes us-
ing ladder MAPs and potential measures. The Lamperti transform has already been
instrumental in uncovering new fluctuation results for the α-stable L´ evy process, and
it is expected to be similarly useful here, provided the challenges of dealing with MAPs
instead of L´ evy processes can be addressed.
In a final example of cpssMps, we will explore in depth how the MAP framework can be
applied to address a specific fluctuation of stable processes: the potential measure for
the isotropic α-stable process L´ evy killed upon entering the infinite strip (0, 1) × Rd−1.
We will highlight the key challenges, which primarily involve handling complicated
integrals, decomposing the paths of MAP as a resurrected MAP with jumps in both
its ordinate and modulator, and understanding the duality of cylindrical MAPs. The
aim with this final example is to provide a preliminary skeleton for future work, to be
refined once these challenges are resolved.
The cylindrically pssMps we will treat are:
1. The ascending ladder process in the direction of X(1), (Ht, X(2:d)+
t )t≥0, given in
equation (2.5).
2. The isotropic α-stable (α ∈ (0, 2) and α = 2) killed on exiting H0↑.
3. The isotropic α-stable α-stable (α ∈ (0, 2) and α = 2) conditioned to stay in the
upper half-space H0↑.
4. The isotropic α-stable α-stable (α ∈ (0, 2) and α = 2) conditioned to hit H0 from
one side.
5. The isotropic α-stable reflected on H0 (α ∈ (0, 2)).
6. The censored isotropic α-stable (α ∈ (0, 2)).
In all the examples the self-similar processes are considered up to the first hit of H0.
This is only relevant if α ∈ (1, 2].
96

We complement this example of cylindrically positive ssMps by studying the case of
Brownian motion, for which many of the expressions included in the fluctuations above
are explicitly known, or easily obtained from the literature.
Killed Brownian Motion as a Cylindrical MAP
Consider X = ( Xt : t ≥ 0) with laws P, a d-dimensional Brownian motion. Let us
define, similarly as before, a Brownian motion killed on exiting H0↑ as the process Y
given by
Yt = Xt1{t<τH0 }, for t ≥ 0,
where τH0 = inf {t > 0 : X(1) = 0 }. It is not difficult to see that Y is a self-similar
Markov process on H0↑. The proof is verbatim to the proof for stable processes at the
102
beginning of Subsection 3.3.2.
Recycling our notation from the previous section, and by virtue of Theorem 3.2, let
us denote by ( ξ∗, Ξ∗) with laws ( P∗
x,θ : x ∈ R, θ ∈ Rd−1) the cylindrical Lamperti-Kiu
transform MAP of Y . This is,
Yt = eξ∗
φ(t)(1, Ξ∗
φ(t)), 0 ≤ t < τ H0.
Note that as Y approaches H0 continuously, the theory of the one-dimensional Lamperti
transform (cf. Section 13.3 of [32]), tells us that ζ ∗
M AP = ∞ and limt→∞ ξ∗
t = −∞. In
fact, this agrees with the fact that q∗ = Γ(α) sin(πα/2)
α is zero if α = 2, and corresponds
to the convention that an exponential random variable of parameter 0 is defined as
+∞.
In the one-dimensional case it is not difficult to show that the Lamperti transform
ξ∗ of Y (1) is a Brownian motion with drift coefficient − 1
2. To see this, consider the
function f(t, x) = e− 1
2 t+x, hence a straightforward application of Itˆ o’s formula tells us
that the process ( f(t, Bt) : t ≥ 0), where ( Bt)t≥0 is a real valued Brownian motion, is
a martingale. Therefore, by the Dambis-Dubins-Schwarz Theorem we have that, for
t < τ H0, the process Y (1) can be written as Y (1)
t = e− 1
2 φ(t)+Bφ(t), where φ(t) = inf{s >
0 :
R s
0 e− 1
2 u+Budu > t }.

For calculating the potential measures we will relate the ladder MAPs in (3.17) and
(3.18) with the ladder processes of Y , or with those of X considering the additional
condition of not being ‘killed’ at H0. We will start by proving a relationship between
local times (at the maximum or minimum) of the cylindrical MAP, and local times at
the directional maxima/minima of X.

It is important to note that the effect that Y being killed has on the potential of
(ξ∗−, Ξ∗−) in the previous calculation, is only that of allowing the logarithm of the spa-
tial transform to be well defined until its lifetime. Intuitively speaking, the descending
ladder of X(1) is the same of Y (1), as long as both processes are positive. However, the
killing plays a more complex role in the ascending ladder potential.
Before giving an explicit formula for the potential of (ξ∗+, Ξ∗+), we present an example
of how said potential can be used in unveiling fluctuations of X.

3.3.4 Stable process conditioned to hit H0 from above
Definition
Consider (X, P) the isotropic α-stable process. We proved in Subsection 2.3.1 that it is
possible to condition the process to hit a set on H0 without visiting H0↓, by equivalently
conditioning on the event where the one-sided point of closest reach hits said set. The
h-transform there is given by (2.58), hence the change of measure is
dP↓
x
dPx

Ft
= (X(1)
t )α/2−1
(x(1))α/2−1 1{t<τ ∨
0 }, x ∈ H0↑, t ≥ 0. (3.28)
Notice that the previous change of measure is trivial in the case α = 2 and take us
back to Brownian Motion killed on exiting H0↑. This occurs because if started in H0↑,
Brownian Motion almost surely hits H0 after staying all its lifetime in H0↑.
It is not difficult to prove that in the case α ∈ (0, 2), the h-transformed process (3.28)
is a cylindrically positive self-similar Markov process and that it has a cylindrical
Lamperti-Kiu representation.
On the cylindrical Williams’ decomposition
Defining P↑ and P↓ suggests the possibility of applying a Williams’ decomposition to
the path of ( X, P), similar to the approach in [29]. Specifically, P can be described up
to its (random) directional minimum in the vertical direction as P↓, and as P↑ after
moving away from this global minimum. However, for the isotropic α-stable process in
H0↑, no such global minimum exists.
Nevertheless, if we lift the restriction to a positive first coordinate and return to the
general cylindrical MAP ( ξ, (J, Ξ)), it was noted in Corollary 3.7 that for α ∈ (0, 1),
there exists a point of minimal distance to H0. This observation hints that the measure
introduced in Section 2.3.2, which is adapted from [28], for X conditioned to hit H0,
might be one of the key components needed to describeP via a Williams’ decomposition.
111
3.3.5 Stable process reflected on H0
Definition
Let us define Z = (Zt : t ≥ 0) the stable process reflected on H0, by the relationship
Zt = (Z(1)
t , Z(2:d)
t ) = (|X(1)

t ) =: R(Xt), t < τ {0},
and Zt = † for t ≥ τ{0}, and equip this process with the laws of the isotropic α-stable
L´ evy process (Px : x ∈ H0↑).
Proving that Z has the scaling property is easy and similar to how we proved it for
the killed process (Subsec. 3.3.2). One can show that if Z[c] is the process given by
Z[c]
t = cZc−αt for t ≥ 0 and some c > 0, then τ [c]
{0} = inf {t > 0 : Z[c](1)
t = 0} = cατ{0},
and the proof follows by the scaling property of X.
For proving the Markov property we will appeal to the isotropy of the transition kernel
of X, pt(x, y), which implies symmetry with respect to H0, i.e., for x, y ∈ Rd
pt((x(1), x(2:d)), (y(1), y(2:d))) = pt((−x(1), x(2:d)), (−y(1), y(2:d))),
and notice that for x ∈ H0↑, t ≥ 0 and any continuous bounded f : H0↑ → R we can
define
Qt[f](x) :=
Z
H0↑

and easily verify that
Qt[f](R(x)) = Pt[f ◦ R](x), (3.29)
where ( Pt)t≥ 0 denotes the semigroup of ( X, P). Hence, on account of R being a
surjective function and by equation (3.29), Lemma 14.1 of [50] affirms that Z is a
Markov process with semigroup ( Qt)t≥0. Moreover, by the continuity of R, the right-
continuity of X, and dominated convergence Z is a Feller process.
Therefore, as Z is a ssMp on E, there exists a cylindrical MAP ( ξR, ΞR) such that for

We will deep dive in how we would attempt to use the recently developed theory of
MAPs (cf. [29]) for finding explicit expressions to the right-hand side of equation (3.39).
There are a number of important facts that will help us to investigate
⇝

⇝
X agree with those of X. The
second fact of interest is that the behaviour of the occupation of the excursions from
the ascending ladder process tells us something about the descending ladder MAP via
duality.

Chapter 4
Numerics of the first entry/exit
of an infinite strip
In this chapter we will consider two problems related to the isotropic α-stable process
in Rd and the infinite strip S = (−1, 1) × Rd−1. The problems consist in finding the
distribution of the position of first entry to S when started in R\S and the distribution
of the first exit of S when started in S. More specifically, if τS = inf {t > 0 : Xt ∈ S}
and τSc = inf{t > 0 : Xt ∈ Rd \ S}, we are interested in the laws
µS
x(dy) := Px(XτS ∈ dy, τS < ∞), y ∈ S, x /∈ S, (4.1)
and
µSc
x (dy) := Px(XτSc ∈ dy), y / ∈ S, x ∈ S. (4.2)
Notice that in the second case, the event {τS < ∞} is omitted due to the oscillatory
behaviour of the first-coordinate process X(1), that is, almost surely
lim sup
t→∞
X(1)
t = − lim inf
t→∞
X(1)
t = +∞,
which guarantees Px(τSc < ∞) = 1.
Our probabilistic motivation to study the problems (4.1) and (4.2) lies in their connec-
tion with a theory of excursions from the minimal distance to the hyperplane H0, and
the promise of obtaining a generalisation of the results of [38] by following an analogous
train of thought to that in said article. In addition to this, there is also an analytic mo-
tivation to solve the same problems. In the analytic jargon, the distributions involved
133
are standardised measures known as the α-harmonic measures, due to their relation
with the Poisson equation for the fractional Laplacian in the domains S and Rd \ S.
There exists a vast literature in regards the two previous problems in the one-dimensional
case. For example, in [6] both problems are solved in the d = 1 isotropic (symmetric)
case. In [55] and [26], problem (4.2) is solved in the d = 1, symmetric case. The
motivation to solve these problems and the approaches they follow vary, but they all
coincide in the fact that the solution satisfies an integral equation, derived by the
Markov property. A decade later, and inspired in [26], the problem (4.2) in dimension
one was solved in [52] for the asymmetric case. More recently, [36] and [48] have found
solutions for the problem (4.1) in the one-dimensional asymmetric case. In the former,
a probabilistic approach using the Lamperti transform of the so-called censored process
is used, whilst in the latter, an integral equation is verified, in a similar spirit to how
it is done in [6].
Extending the results on hitting and exiting S from d = 1 to d ≥ 2 is highly non-trivial.
For instance, if we attempt to follow the approach in [6], which involves solving an
integral equation, the resulting integral equations—solved through an educated guess
of the solutions—require the verification of rather difficult integrals, which are difficult
to solve explicitly. An alternative, more accessible route is the probabilistic method
introduced in [36], which begins by defining a censored process in higher dimensions.
While setting up such a process is relatively straightforward, expressing it in a form
suitable for practical computations again leads to complicated integrals, similar to those
encountered in the analytic approach.
The aim of this chapter is to use numerical methods in order to understand the distri-
butions in problems (4.1) and (4.2). In particular we will develop sampling algorithms
which theoretically converge to a sampling algorithm of the distribution of the first hit
into/out S.
4.1 Simulating the first hit into the strip
The aim of this section is to give a partial distributional decomposition of the density of
the first hit into the infinite slab S = (−1, 1) × Rd−1 of (X, P), together with a Monte
Carlo method for numerically determining it. Pre-emptively assuming it exists, the
density we are interested in is the bivariate function H(x, y) such that for x ∈ Rd \ S,
Px(XτS ∈ dy, τS < ∞) = H(x, y)dy, y ∈ (−1, 1) × Rd−1.
134
It is known that in the case α ∈ (1, 2) the process X will hit the slab with probability
one because X(1), its first coordinate, will hit any interval with probability one; cf.
[36]. However, in the case α ∈ (0, 1), we must necessarily work on the event {τS < ∞}.
Because stable processes always make first passage into a half space by a jump (i.e. no
creeping) we know that the coordinate X(1) must make a random number of crossings
of (−1, 1) before entering it. To this end, without loss of generality, we can start with
x(1) > 1 and define σ0 = 0. Then for k = 1, 2, · · ·, iteratively define,

4.1.2 Solving the theoretical non-convergence of the case α ∈ (0, 1)
One of the problems with Algorithm 1 is that, in the setting α ∈ (0, 1), the while loop
may never end on account of the fact that, by Corollary 1.2 of [36], Px(τS = ∞) > 0.
Nonetheless, it is possible to ‘force’ a stable process to hit S in the case α ∈ (0, 1)
improving the efficiency of the walk-on-half-spaces algorithm without compromising
the exactness of the algorithm. Such technique consists in conditioning the process to
hit the hyperplane H0. This is done by considering the h-transform h(x) = |x(1)|α−1
for x ∈ Rd (cf. Section 2.3.2). Let us call P◦ to such conditional law, for which now is
clear that P◦
x(τS < ∞) = 1 or in other words
P◦
x(XS ∈ dy, τS < ∞) = P◦
x(XS ∈ dy).
We can calculate the distribution of the first hit into a half-space under P◦ just multi-

4.2 Simulating the first exit from the strip
To simulate a realisation from the kernel µSc
x (dy), x ∈ S,y /∈ S, it is possible to use the
Walk-on-spheres algorithm [33].
The algorithm (see Figure 4-3) consists in sampling from the first exit of the biggest
sphere centered in x and inscribed in S, if the sample is still in the strip, then we
sample the first exit of the biggest sphere centered in the previous realisation. Once
the sample exits the strip, this is equivalent to a realisation from µSc
x . It is easy to
see that the biggest sphere inscribed in the strip, and centered in x will always have
a radius equal to the minimum between 1 − x(1), the distance from x to the upper
boundary of the strip, and x(1) − (−1), the distance from x to the bottom lid of the
strip. Consider τ ∧
Br(x) := {t > 0 : |Xt − x| > r } the first exit of the ball centered in x
and with radius r.
Algorithm 2 (simulation of XτSc under Px for x ∈ Rd with x(1) ∈ (−1, 1).
set r0 = min{1 − x(1), x(1) + 1}
sample x1 from the distribution of Xτ ∧
Br0
(x) under Px;
set k = 1;
while xk ∈ S:
set rk = min{1 − x(1)
k , x(1)
k + 1};
sample xk+1 from the distribution of Xτ ∧
Brk (xk )
under Pxk;
set: k = k + 1;
end while
return N = k;
return XτSc = xk.

4.2.3 Cauchy representation of the first exit from the strip
Let us assume preemptively that a density forµSc
x (dy) with respect to Lebesgue measure
exists, i.e.

4.2.4 More about the WoS and Monte Carlo simulation
In principle, although it was not originally formulated for unbounded sets, one could
use the walk-on-spheres method for sampling the first hit into the slab S = (−1, 1) × R.
However, even realising this, one of the reasons to prefer the walk-on-half-spaces algo-
rithm over the WoS is its improved efficiency. For example, considerx = (x(1), 0, . . . ,0)
above the slab, i.e. x(1) > 1, the probability that the WoS requires only one simulation
150
to get inside the slab is
Px(XτBx(1)−1(x) ∈ S) = P
 
XτB1(0) ∈

Cα,d|1 − |y|2|−α/2|x − y|−ddy, (4.17)
where Cα,d = π−(d/2+1)Γ(d/2) sin(απ/2) and in the first equality we used shifting and
scaling as in Lemma 4.4. This integral is bounded by 1/2, due to the 1/2 probability
of exiting the ball Bx(1)−1(x) by its upper hemisphere.
On the other hand, in the WoHS algorithm, the probability of entering the slab in the
first jump is given by
Px(1)(X(1)

It is not obvious that the previous probability is always greater than the integral (4.17),
however, we can compare these expressions numerically as functions of x(1) and for
different values ofα (see Fig.4-7), and conclude that for this domain the WoHS produces
a first simulation faster than WoS.

Probability of hitting the slab in the first step of WoHS vs WoS
WoHS = 0.1

Figure 4-7: Comparison of the rate of acceptance of the first jump in WoHS
vs WoS for simulating the first hit into the slab.
Notice in Fig.4-7 that there is a monotonic behaviour in terms ofα for the walk-on-half-
spaces algorithm, whilst for the WoS when α gets closer to 2 the probability of hitting
151
the slab decreases significantly. This occurs because for α ≈ 2 the law of XτBx(1)−1(x)
is highly concentrated near the boundary of the ball, making that the only likely point
to be hit near the slab is the tangent point between slab and sphere. In closing this
chapter on numerical methods for determining the distribution
Px(XτD ∈ dy | τD < ∞), x / ∈ D, y ∈ D,
where τD = inf {t > 0 : Xt ∈ D} for a suitably well-behaved domain D, we emphasize
the potential of hybrid approaches, such as combining the Walk-on-Spheres (WoS) and
Walk-on-Halfspaces (WoHS) methods, to improve computational efficiency.
Several avenues for further exploration arise naturally from this work. First, can we
rigorously establish that the WoHS method provides an unbiased estimate of the solu-
tion to a fractional Dirichlet problem? While similar results have been shown in the
context of non-fractional cases (e.g., α = 2), extending these proofs to fractional set-
tings presents challenges. Notably, the existing results by Kyprianou et al. rely heavily
on Bucur’s work [11], which is constrained to scenarios involving exiting balls. Broad-
ening these proofs to more general domains remains an open question of significant
theoretical interest.
Additionally, the notion of computational efficiency warrants further clarification, par-
ticularly through practical examples. For instance, consider a domain shaped like a
puzzle piece, characterized by a combination of flat and concave edges. Accurately and
efficiently estimating the distribution of first hitting points on such a complex domain
underscores the practical utility of the combined WoS and WoHS methods, not only in
the fractional case, but also in the classical Dirichlet problem (case α = 2).
These considerations highlight the intersection of theory and computational innovation,
setting the stage for future work to refine and expand the applicability of these methods
to broader classes of domains and stochastic processes.
